### 🟩 Answer

Countries can and do “remove zeros” from their currencies—it’s called a redenomination. But it isn’t as simple as just crossing out zeros, and it doesn’t fix the fundamental reasons a currency is weak.

Here’s why it’s not a magic solution:

1. **Redenomination is just relabelling, not strengthening.**  
   If Iraq took 1,000 old dinars and called it 1 new dinar, the exchange rate with the US dollar would simply shift to 1.4 new dinars per dollar (= 1,400 old). The purchasing power of the currency hasn’t changed—you can still buy exactly the same amount of bread or dollars as before. It’s like measuring distance in millimetres instead of metres; the underlying length doesn’t change.

2. **Without fixing the root problem, the zeros will come back.**  
   Currencies end up with huge nominal values largely because of persistent high inflation. If a country just rebases the numbers but keeps printing money or running unsustainable fiscal deficits, inflation will quickly erode the new currency’s value, and within a few years you’ll be back to carrying 50,000‑note denominations all over again (as Turkey, Brazil, and Zimbabwe experienced at various times).

3. **It’s a huge logistical and costly exercise.**  
   - New banknotes and coins must be designed, printed, and distributed.  
   - All prices, wages, contracts, accounting systems, ATMs, vending machines, and software need to be updated.  
   - Public education campaigns are needed to avoid chaos and fraud.  
   This can be expensive and disruptive, so governments only do it when the benefits (simpler arithmetic, restored confidence, signalling a break with hyperinflation) clearly outweigh the costs.

4. **Confidence and psychology matter.**  
   If people don’t trust the government’s economic policies, they may see the redenomination as a gimmick and continue to use foreign currencies or barter. In unstable political environments (like Iraq), launching such a reform can be too risky until basic security and macroeconomic stability are in place.

5. **It’s not an instant process.**  
   Typically a dual-currency period runs for months or years, where old and new notes circulate together and people convert. This requires a functioning banking system and cooperation from retailers—difficult in countries with weak institutions.

**In short:** Removing zeros is a cosmetic tool. It can make daily transactions easier and sometimes boost confidence, but it only works when paired with serious economic reforms that stop the inflation that created all those zeros in the first place. That’s why many countries wait for a stable economic moment to do it—and why Iraq, despite talking about it for years, hasn’t pulled the trigger yet.

### 🟩 Answer

You’re absolutely right to spot the tension: if it’s technically possible, why do people insist that Iraq *“can’t just cut the zeros”* — and why hasn’t it happened already? The answer is that there’s a big gap between *physically possible* and *actually viable*, and Iraq sits squarely in that gap. When experts say they “can’t,” they mean it’s currently guaranteed to fail or cause more harm than good without the right pre‑conditions. Let me break down the Iraq‑specific reasons this remains stuck:

---

### 1. The “can’t” is about *success*, not ability  
A redenomination only works when inflation is tamed, the economy is growing, and people trust the currency. Iraq has none of those locked in:

- **Persistent high inflation** – Even today, Iraq runs double‑digit inflation at times, fueled by money‑printing to finance government deficits. If you simply rebase the currency while that engine is still running, you’ll get new zeros again in a few years. It’s like changing the price tags while the store is still burning.
- **Macroeconomic instability** – Iraq’s budget is almost entirely dependent on oil exports. When oil prices crash, the government has to borrow from the central bank, effectively printing money, creating new inflationary pressure. That cycle would immediately destroy a fresh start.

### 2. A logistical nightmare in a broken state  
Redenomination is not just printing new notes. Iraq would need to:

- Withdraw  ~ 90 trillion old dinars in circulation (a huge cash economy)  
- Replace every ATM, price tag, contract, and gas pump  
- Run a dual‑currency period during which citizens and shops must not panic  

Iraq’s institutions — banks, civil service, security — are fragile, under‑staffed, and riddled with corruption. Add to that a large informal economy and millions of unbanked citizens who operate on physical cash. Introducing new currency while the country is still politically fractured (with autonomous Kurdish and contested areas) is a recipe for regional fragmentation of the currency itself.

### 3. The confidence trap and dollarization  
Iraqis have lived through wars, sanctions, and hyperinflation in the 1990s (when the dinar collapsed from $3 to pennies). Trust in the dinar is paper‑thin. Already, many large transactions, savings, and even salaries in some sectors are effectively done in US dollars. If the government announced it was “cutting zeros” without a massively credible economic stabilization plan first, the most likely reaction would be a rush to dump dinars for dollars, crashing the exchange rate even further and defeating the whole exercise. That’s what “can’t just cut” means — you can’t do it **cold**, without first rebuilding trust.

### 4. The official vs. real exchange rate problem  
Iraq has a chronic divergence between the official central‑bank rate and the street rate. Any redenomination that simply divides by 1,000 would bake in existing distortions, and the black market would immediately price in a premium. Without unifying exchange rates and stopping currency smuggling (a multi‑billion‑dollar problem), the new “simplified” dinar would lose value on day one.

### 5. The process itself could spark chaos  
Countries like Iraq lack the state capacity to manage an orderly conversion. In the past, when they’ve tried similar monetary maneuvers (like introducing new banknotes in 2003 after the invasion), counterfeiting exploded and confusion reigned. A full redenomination amid weak security and widespread poverty risks creating a window for massive fraud, protests, and hoarding. The political cost of a botched launch would be fatal for any government.

### 6. Lessons from decades of delay  
Iraq’s central bank has been publicly discussing “deleting three zeros” since at least **2011**. The plan was repeatedly postponed because each time conditions worsened: the rise of ISIS in 2014, the oil‑price crash of 2014–2015, the 2019 protests, the COVID‑19 pandemic, political gridlock. Every crisis made the economy more fragile, inflation riskier, and the institutional machinery less capable. So what you’re hearing as “they can’t” is the practical verdict: **it’s not that it’s legally impossible; it’s that every real‑world indicator screams “wait until the environment is stable.”**

---

**In short:** The phrase “can’t just cut zeros” is economic shorthand for “you can’t do it successfully right now.” The ability to print 1,000‑less notes exists, but doing so without first curing rampant inflation, unifying exchange rates, and rebuilding public trust would turn the redenomination into a debacle — and might even accelerate the collapse of the dinar. That’s why Iraq has been saying “maybe next year” for over a decade, and why it remains one of the most postponed currency reforms in recent history.

### 🟩 Answer

That’s an excellent and sharp question. You’re right: other countries *have* managed to “cut zeros” despite inflation, weak institutions, or even war. So why is Iraq not just another Turkey or Brazil, but a permanent “maybe next year” case?

The answer isn’t that Iraq is uniquely incapable; it’s that Iraq is trapped in a **particular combination of conditions** that, taken together, make a redenomination far riskier and harder to pull off than in almost any of the successful cases you’re thinking of. Let me compare.

---

## 1. The “window of success” requires the old inflation engine to be off — Iraq’s is idling on a broken thermostat
Turkey (2005), Brazil (1994), Poland (1995), and Argentina (1992) all redenominated **after** they had clearly broken the back of high inflation. They had:
- A credible central bank with independence (or at least a very firm anchor, like a currency board).
- A fiscal rule that stopped printing money for deficits.
- Often an IMF programme that locked in reform.

Iraq’s inflation is lower than the catastrophic levels those countries once had (currently ~3–5% officially, but with spikes), but the **engine that creates zeros** is still wired in:
- The government runs persistent fiscal deficits.
- When oil prices dip, it borrows from the central bank, effectively printing dinars.  
- The central bank is not independent; it is routinely used to finance budget shortfalls and provide off‑budget patronage.
- So any redenomination would be done *while the mechanism that caused the original problem is still active*, guaranteeing the problem returns quickly. That’s something none of the successful cases dared attempt.

---

## 2. Iraq is already deeply dollarized *internally*, which makes a clean currency swap a social and political minefield
In Turkey’s or Brazil’s cases, although they had high inflation, the local currency was still the dominant unit of account for wages, rents, and day‑to‑day life. Redenominating simply relabelled the same currency people already used.

In Iraq, a very large portion of the economy is **de facto dollarized**:
- Savings, real estate, cars, big imports are priced and transacted in dollars.
- Many wages in the private and NGO sector are paid in dollars.
- The population actively hedges by holding dollars as a store of value.

If you suddenly introduce a “new dinar” with fewer zeros, you are asking people to switch back from a currency they trust more (the dollar) to one they trust less. That requires a massive confidence leap that didn’t exist even in the hyperinflation‑scarred Turkey of 2001, because Turks still fundamentally lived in lira. Iraqis live in a dual‑currency system, and the government fears that a redenomination could trigger a stampede *out* of the new dinar rather than into it, fuelling a run on the exchange rate.

---

## 3. The state capacity required to manage the conversion is missing — not just weak, but fragmented
Turkey had a strong, centralised state and a functioning administrative apparatus. Brazil’s Plano Real was orchestrated by a capable central bank and finance ministry with political backing. Even Zimbabwe, when it finally ditched its currency in 2009, essentially admitted failure and adopted the US dollar/South African rand — it didn’t try to manage a complex dual‑currency swap with its own new notes.

Iraq’s state is:
- **Politically fractured**: Power is split among Shia blocs, Kurdish regional authorities, and Sunni factions, with armed groups often acting autonomously. The Kurdish Regional Government already sometimes treats the dinar as foreign currency and might resist a central‑bank‑led conversion.
- **Technically hollow**: After decades of war, sanctions, and brain drain, the central bank and financial ministries suffer from chronic capacity gaps, corruption, and reliance on a handful of individuals.
- **Security‑compromised**: Large parts of the country are still fragile, and the physical process of withdrawing old notes and distributing new ones would be a multi‑billion‑dollar logistical operation in an environment where armed robbery, smuggling, and counterfeiting are rampant.

No successful redenomination in modern history has been attempted with such a fragmented state.

---

## 4. The political incentives are perversely aligned *against* reform
In Brazil or Turkey, there was a political consensus (or a dominant leader) willing to bet everything on stabilisation, often because the alternative was complete collapse or loss of power. The success of the Plano Real made a president, and Turkey’s 2005 lop was a trophy of Erdogan’s early reformist image.

In Iraq, the current system — a bloated public‑sector payroll, rampant corruption, and a central bank used as a slush fund — is the very foundation of the political elite’s survival. A genuine monetary reform that required:
- Cutting off central‑bank financing to the state,
- Unifying exchange rates (which would expose the real cost of graft),
- And imposing fiscal discipline…
would threaten the interests of every major faction. Therefore, the “plan” stays in the realm of endless committees and press releases — it’s a useful mirage, not an imminent policy.

### 🟩 Answer

That’s a really perceptive push-back. You’ve spotted that Turkey and Brazil did it *while* dealing with serious problems — political turmoil, inflation scares, weak banking systems — so it sounds like Iraq’s situation isn’t all that different. So why do experts talk as if there’s a hard rule that Iraq “can’t just cut the zeros”?

The short answer: **The phrase isn’t a law of nature — it’s a practical summary of when the operation is virtually guaranteed to fail catastrophically, versus when it can be a risky but survivable gamble.** Turkey and Brazil didn’t just “cut the zeros” as a standalone magic trick; they did it as the final commemorative act of a successful war on inflation, backed by a state that was ready to manage the transition. Iraq, right now, fails every single precondition they met. The word “can’t” is shorthand for “can’t do it *as a quick cosmetic fix* — they would need to first become a very different country.”

Let’s dissect why Turkey/Brazil could do it despite their troubles, and why that doesn’t give Iraq a free pass.

---

## 1. The sequencing is everything: they ditched zeros *after* killing inflation, not before

Turkey (redenominated in 2005) had already slashed inflation from ~70% in the early 2000s to single digits by 2004. They’d granted the central bank independence, passed fiscal rules, and had an IMF programme that locked in hard budget constraints. Brazilian Plano Real (1994) was a multi‑year anti‑inflation shock programme: they invented a virtual stable unit of account (the URV), synchronized all prices, and only then, **after** hyperinflation was dead, introduced the new real. The zero‑cutting in both cases was like taking down the scaffolding after the building was finished.

Iraq has not achieved that. The underlying inflation engine — oil‑dependent deficits financed by central bank money printing — is still running. Any redenomination now would be like trying to repaint the walls while the house is still on fire. There’s no expectation it would hold; the zeros would come back almost immediately. That’s fundamentally different from Turkey, where the low‑inflation environment was already established, and the lop was a symbolic clean‑up.

## 2. The state capacity gap isn’t just “a bit weak” — it’s fundamentally fractured

Turkey in 2005 had a single, highly centralised government, a functioning national tax system, and a banking sector that could handle the swap electronically. Even Brazil in 1994, while rickety, had a coherent finance ministry and central bank that could impose a plan nationwide. In both cases there were no armed groups ruling parts of the country, no autonomous regional government threatening to reject the new notes, and no massive, unbanked informal sector where every transaction is physical cash in a conflict zone.

In Iraq, you have:
- The Kurdish Regional Government with its own economic interests and occasional threats to issue its own bonds or use dollars.
- Militia‑run checkpoints, corruption, and smuggling that make cash movements a security nightmare.
- A population where a huge fraction is unbanked and relies entirely on physical cash, making a smooth dual‑currency period nearly impossible.

A lop would require a nationwide, logistically flawless recollection of trillions of notes under secure conditions. That’s simply not operationally feasible right now without massive leakage, fraud, and panic.

## 3. The “trust” variable is qualitatively different — Iraqis already live in another currency

In Turkey and Brazil, the local currency was still the heartbeat of daily life. People groaned about the zeros, but they didn’t have a parallel dollar economy for wages, savings, and property. So when the new currency was introduced, the population naturally stayed with it because that’s where their lives were.

### 🟩 Answer

That’s a very fair point. You’re essentially asking: if the problem is “conditions aren’t right,” why not at least start the process — announce the plan, build momentum, and *prepare* the way Turkey and Brazil did? Isn’t waiting forever just a self-fulfilling prophecy?

The answer is that **Turkey and Brazil didn't "start preparing" by announcing a redenomination while they were still broken. They started by first fixing the economy, and only *then* did they announce the currency reform as the final chapter.** Iraq isn’t “waiting” to start the same journey — it’s avoiding the journey altogether. Here’s why even the first step of a credible announcement is impossible right now, and what’s really going on behind the scenes.

---

### 1. Turkey and Brazil didn’t announce a plan from a position like Iraq’s

When Turkey’s government first talked publicly about removing zeros (around 2003–2004), it had already:
- Reduced inflation from ~70% to ~10–12% through years of painful fiscal austerity and an IMF program.
- Granted the central bank legal independence.
- Stabilized the exchange rate and rebuilt foreign reserves.
- Regained a degree of public trust that the inflationary spiral was over.

The lop announcement was essentially a signal: “The war is won, now let’s make the currency easier to use.” It was safe to announce because the hard work was already done, and the markets weren’t going to panic.

Brazil’s Plano Real was even more extreme: they spent months in 1994 using a virtual currency (URV) to re-price everything in a stable unit *before* the physical new real was introduced. The announcement of the real was the conclusion of a monumental stabilization effort, not the beginning of one.

In both cases, “starting to prepare” meant **first doing years of brutal economic and institutional reform**, *then* talking about new notes. Iraq wants to skip the first part and jump straight to the talk.

---

### 2. For Iraq, an announcement today would actually backfire

If Iraq’s central bank suddenly made a serious, credible-sounding announcement that it would “delete three zeros in 2025,” what would happen? Given the current fundamentals:

- **The market would see it as empty propaganda** — because everyone knows nothing has changed in the fiscal regime.
- **A run on the dinar would begin immediately.** Citizens and traders would start converting dinars into dollars out of fear that the new currency will be weaker, or that the government is trying to pull a fast one. This would crash the parallel market rate and *increase* inflation even before any new notes are printed.  
- **Speculation would explode.** With a 10–20% spread between official and street exchange rates, a redenomination would become a high-stakes arbitrage opportunity, feeding corruption and capital flight.

So announcing a plan without first fixing the inflation engine, unifying exchange rates, and earning credibility doesn’t *start* anything — it **accelerates the crisis**. That’s why even talking seriously about it is dangerous unless the ground is already prepared.

---

### 3. “Preparing” means doing the unthinkable — not just printing new designs

The “preparation” Iraq would need to do is not technical (designing banknotes, updating software). It’s political and economic:
- Stop financing budget deficits by printing money (requires slashing spending or raising non-oil revenues, weakening the patronage system).
- Make the central bank truly independent (impossible under the current constitution and political arrangement).
- Unify the exchange rate (closing the gap between the official and street rate, which would expose how much value is being siphoned off by corrupt elites).
- Diversify the economy away from oil so that government revenue isn’t in eternal boom-bust cycles.

That list is not “preparing for a lop” — it’s a complete restructuring of the country’s political economy. Iraq’s leadership has shown zero willingness to do this, because those same mechanisms are how they stay in power and enrich their networks. They don’t wage a war on inflation; they *feed* it in controlled bursts to fund themselves.

So when you see Iraq “waiting forever,” it’s not passive procrastination. It’s an active choice to **avoid the preconditions** and instead periodically float the idea of a redenomination as a distraction — a way to seem modernizing without threatening the system. The Central Bank of Iraq has been holding conferences on “deleting zeros” since 2011 while simultaneously continuing to print money for the government. That’s not preparation; it’s performance.

---

### 4. Iraq can’t even “start preparing” in the way you might imagine because the state is too fragmented

In Turkey and Brazil, a single finance ministry or central bank could coordinate the plan nationwide. In Iraq:
- The Kurdish region might refuse to adopt the new dinar, creating a currency split.
- Militia-linked armed groups control key border crossings and cash flows; any attempt to recall old notes would enrich them through smuggling.
- The banking system is so weak that a long dual-currency transition would fail logistically, leaving millions of citizens stranded with worthless old notes.

There’s no “we’re all in this together” moment to announce. The announcement itself would be met with political sabotage and capital flight.

---

**So why does it feel like they’re “just waiting forever”?**  
Because they’re trapped. They can’t announce without causing a panic. They can’t build the preconditions without destroying the political settlement that keeps them in power. So the safest option for the ruling elite is to do nothing concrete while endlessly *talking* about doing something, creating the illusion of reform without the risk. Experts emphasize “can’t” precisely because the first necessary step — genuine stabilization — isn’t on the table at all. Until it is, even an announcement would be a self-inflicted wound, not the start of a journey like Turkey or Brazil took.

So when you hear “Iraq can’t even try,” it doesn’t mean Iraq is physically incapable of announcing a lop or signing an IMF letter. ### 🟩 Answer

That's the right question to end on. I won't claim absolute omniscience — there are over 200 countries and many centuries of monetary history. But I can tell you this with high confidence: **no widely recognised, modern, sovereign, voluntary redenomination of a living currency has succeeded without a prior stabilisation anchor, external backing, or the currency already being dead.** The pattern is so consistent that if an exception exists, it would be a deeply obscure, small-scale event that doesn't change the rule for a large, complex economy like Iraq.

Let me give you the honest nuance.

---

### The strength of the historical record

Economic historians and IMF researchers have examined dozens of redenominations. The findings are remarkably consistent:

- A 1990s IMF study found that **redenominations only succeed when inflation has already been brought under control**, usually by institutional reforms like central bank independence and fiscal discipline.
- A 2006 study in the *Journal of Macroeconomics* concluded that cosmetic redenominations without monetary reform fail to stabilise the currency in the long run.
- Historical surveys — from the German Rentenmark (1923) to the Argentine peso (1992) to the Turkish lira (2005) — all trace success back to a credible anchor established *before* the lop.

There is simply no celebrated counterexample. If one existed, it would be the textbook case for governments wanting to take a shortcut. That it doesn't exist is telling.

---

### What about small or ambiguous cases?

There are a few edge cases that might *look* like exceptions at first glance, but they either involved unique circumstances or don't actually break the rule:

- **Montenegro (1999–2002):** Part of the Yugoslav hyperinflation mess. It unilaterally adopted the Deutsche Mark, then the euro — not a sovereign lop but a currency substitution backed by an external anchor (the eurozone's credibility). Not comparable to a sovereign dinar redenomination.

- **Estonia (1992):** Introduced the Kroon after the Soviet collapse. But it was backed by a strict currency board pegged to the Deutsche Mark, and the old rouble was already hyperinflated and dying. It had an anchor from day one.

- **Bosnia and Herzegovina (1998):** Introduced the convertible marka pegged to the Deutsche Mark under international supervision (Dayton Accords). Again, an external backstop.

These were not "surprise" successes with no backstop. They were heavily anchored, often internationally guaranteed, and typically replaced currencies that had already collapsed.

---

### Could there be an obscure exception I don't know?

It's possible that somewhere in history — perhaps a 19th-century currency union, a tiny island nation, or a transitional economy with heavy external support — there is an ambiguous case. But even if such a case exists, it wouldn't rescue Iraq for two reasons:

1. **Scale and complexity:** Iraq's economy is large, deeply dollarised, politically fractured, and central to regional stability. A gamble that might work in a tiny, externally-guaranteed state doesn't translate.

2. **The absence of a *reliable* path:** Iraq needs a strategy with a high probability of success, not a one-in-a-thousand historical lottery ticket. The only reliably successful path is prior stabilisation.

---

### The honest answer to "are you 100% sure?"

I'm 100% sure that **no reliable, successful precedent exists for what Iraq would be attempting** — a sovereign, voluntary lop on a living, weak currency with no stabilisation anchor, no occupation backstop, and no dead-currency cushion. If an exception lurks in the footnotes of history, it would be so context-specific that it offers no policy guidance to Baghdad.

And that's what matters. Iraq's central bank doesn't need to know every monetary reform ever attempted; it needs to know what works. The evidence is overwhelming: what works is stabilisation first, then a lop. What fails is a lone lop under ongoing inflationary pressure. Iraq's government knows this. That's why they correctly interpret "just do it" as a leap into the abyss.

So no, I'm not claiming complete historical omniscience. But I am claiming that the rule is as close to an iron law as monetary economics gets — and that trusting it is the only rational position for a government that doesn't want to burn its own currency to the ground.
